## Beware of False Alchemists

The author speaks: I also do not wish to mention here the great defects that occur in medicine, wherein in all places physicians and experienced doctors—for whom nothing could be more harmful or shameful in these current times—find their profession and office encroached upon by old, washed-out, superstitious women, a stinking bath-assistant <note>German: *Baderknecht*; an assistant to a barber-surgeon who performed lowly tasks like cupping or bathing.</note>, a bold and daring barber <note>German: *Bartscherer*; in this era, barbers also acted as surgeons, often with little formal medical training.</note>, an unbelieving and Christian-deceiving Jew, a talkative sophist and deceiver, a liar, and finally those who pass themselves off as shameless Paracelsists <note>Followers of Paracelsus (1493–1541). While Paracelsus was a pioneer of chemical medicine, his fame led many frauds to claim his name to sell unreliable "spagyric" cures.</note>. I must indeed allow it to happen that often a false alchemist <term>Alchymist</term> sets someone up with lies and fraud, distilling the money out of another's pocket and carrying some of it away. Let every seeker <note>German: *Liebhaber*, literally "lover" of the art; a common term for a dedicated student of alchemy.</note> guard himself against such as these.

### Therefore, the arts should not be rejected due to abuse

For if one were to judge an art and doctrine by its abuse, then one would have to reject Jurisprudence <term>Jurisprudentiam</term> <note>The study and theory of law.</note>, Medicine, and Theology. Have we not often seen and experienced in our times that tailors, furriers, shoemakers, and some lowly agi-

If Alchemy <term>Alchimy</term> <note>The early modern precursor to chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the search for the "Philosopher's Stone."</note> is a true art and a mother of such great gifts and usefulness, how does it happen that no great lord, who has hitherto applied himself to this art and still persists in it, achieves the desired goal?

## Why Princes and Lords
### do not attain the final end of this art—

This question is indeed worthy of being resolved. But when one reflects properly upon the matter, it is easy to answer.

### The First Cause.

Daily experience shows that often sensible and learned people err and fail in small matters, and even neglect their own affairs; therefore, it is no wonder that princes and lords, who are always burdened with important and great matters, fail in the investigation of this difficult art—which far surpasses human reason and understanding unless the spirit is enlightened by **GOD**—as this...

<summary>This page addresses the skeptical question of why powerful and wealthy rulers fail to master alchemy if the art is indeed genuine. The author begins his defense by arguing that worldly burdens and a lack of divine enlightenment often prevent even the most learned and powerful from succeeding in such a complex spiritual and physical endeavor.</summary>

->❦ ❦ ❦<-

masters of alchemy write of it—[they] must err and stumble. They indeed have enough learned people, but they do not take them into counsel, and do not use them correctly; either because they do not hold the art in high regard, relying instead on their treasures, income, and physicians—whom they alone trust—and do not pursue the art with diligence, nor do they have a proper goal or measure in it; or they perhaps even despise the art because many erroneous, difficult, and seductive paths have been presented and prescribed by the philosophers <term>Philosophis</term> <note>original: Philosophis; referring to the "natural philosophers" or ancient alchemical authorities.</note> and masters of this art. Therefore, when a sophist <term>Sophist</term> <note>A term often used in alchemical texts to describe a charlatan or a "false brother" who uses deceptive logic and trickery rather than true science.</note> and deceiver <term>Betrieger</term> often approaches them, who can claim great things and makes the art seem easy, and is perhaps recommended by a court-friend <term>Hoff-freund</term> <note>A courtier or sycophant who uses their influence with a prince to promote favorites.</note>, such a person is immediately given service and pay—much sooner than a learned man and a knowledgeable philosopher <term>Philofopho</term> <note>original: Philofopho; meaning a true practitioner of the Hermetic arts.</note> who is experienced in the art.

### The Second Cause.

Secondly, what potentate <term>Potentat</term> <note>A monarch or powerful ruler.</note> has ever desired the counsel of the learned and wise in this great and important secret <term>Geheimnus</term> <note>The "Secret" or "Mystery," referring to the Great Work of alchemy.</note>, who have prescribed a way to learn and attain the art, or shown the means by which one might reach the art’s end? They have pursued this art more for the sake of pleasure, and have not taken it so seriously to heart, nor wished to apply such diligence as the experience of this art requires and demands.

<summary>The author explains that rulers fail at alchemy because they trust charlatans and courtiers over true scholars, and because they treat the art as a mere hobby rather than a rigorous pursuit requiring deep commitment.</summary>
<keywords>charlatans, court life, alchemical education, patronage, sophists, potentates, the Great Work</keywords>

## The Third Cause.

If there has been a prince or lord who made use of a learned man's instruction and teaching, that person <note>Referring to the advisor or servant already in the prince's service.</note> was perhaps also an evil man—as, unfortunately, the majority of people are inclined—seeing more to their own profit and gain than to their lords and princes. They leave it to chance <note>original: "schlagens in die Schantz"; a phrase meaning to take a risk or leave something to fortune, often with a sense of gambling with another's resources.</note> how long he might live and remain a prince, or how long [his] favor might last. (Ah, unfortunately there are still such evil hearts today; but whomever GOD has chosen for this, no devil shall rob.) Therefore, if any experienced, practiced <note>The catchword from the previous page, "übe," refers to "geübter" (practiced/expert) here.</note>, and learned <term>Alchemist</term> <note>Alchymist; a practitioner of the chemical and Hermetic arts.</note> desires to present himself to his Grace, the former [servant] becomes anxious that if this newcomer should succeed, he himself might lose his position. Consequently, he hinders and slanders the newcomer so that he cannot be admitted or come forward. Thus, the dear prince and lord is left without good counsel <term>Counsel</term> <note>original: Raht; meaning both advice and the body of advisors.</note> and is obstructed; he must rely on his servant's thoughts and be satisfied with them, and afterwards—according to the old proverb—he finds the hoped-for treasure in coals <note>original: "Schatz in Kohlen"; a common idiomatic expression for alchemical failure, where a process yields only burnt residue rather than gold.</note>.

## The Fourth Cause.

Furthermore, everyone knows well how sleepily and negligently one conducts another's business and manages it, when one has reason to fear that they might receive disfavor for favor, imprisonment for good deeds, and for freedom <term>Freedom</term> <note>original: Freyheit; often referring to the legal status and privileges of a person at court.</note> the loss of the same—

<summary>This page describes the political obstacles to alchemy, specifically how jealous courtiers block genuine experts from reaching the prince to protect their own status, and how fear of punishment leads to servant negligence.</summary>
<keywords>corruption, court life, jealousy, alchemical failure, Treasure in Coals, political intrigue, counsel</keywords>

...should receive and attain. For who can and will believe that in this evil time of the world, in which every man wishes to be a master and all <term>heresy</term> <note>original: Ketzerey; here likely referring to general social and religious upheaval or deviation from established truth.</note> is in full swing, that a prince would safely grant freedom to the person through whose diligence, toil, and labor he had reached the successful completion of the Art <note>The "Art" (Kunst) refers to the alchemical process of transmutation or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>? For he <note>The practitioner or alchemist.</note> would always be in such thought and fear that such a great work would be poorly rewarded, that he would be obligated to be an eternal servant, or would face the danger that if he were pursued with treachery, he would be forced to reveal such a secret to others as well. Therefore he thinks it better for him that the Lord remain always in ignorance, and daily involve and entangle himself ever more in the Art, provided that his <term>salary</term> <note>original: Besoldung; the regular pay or stipend provided to a court official.</note> alone remains, and he obtains his table and upkeep richly from it, and otherwise casts everything else to the wind <note>original: schlaget sonst alles in Wind; an idiom meaning to disregard or ignore something completely.</note>.

### The Fifth Cause.

Therefore, I also firmly believe that if someone had attained the Art through a prince's expense—notwithstanding that he was obligated to do so—he would immediately remove himself from service and make away. As happened with a <term>Doctor of Medicine</term> <note>original: Doctor Medicinæ; a university-trained physician.</note>, who was a German by birth and language, when he had been in a prince's <term>appointment</term> <note>original: Bestallung; a formal commission or contract of employment at court.</note> for some time and, through grace, books, and expenses, as well as many experiences, finally...

## The First Story of Transmutation.

->**On separating silver from the Lazuli stone**<-

Shortly thereafter, a conversation arose—though I do not know how they came upon the subject—that someone had dared, through the alchemical art <term>Alchymistische Kunst|The precursor to modern chemistry, alchemy sought to understand the properties of matter and achieve transmutations.</term>, to produce and separate silver from the Lazuli stone. The Scottish nobleman bites his lips and leaves the apothecary shop in anger; he returns to his host and tells him how things had gone for him, and decides with him how he wants to vex the apothecary and "show him the ape" <note>original: "den Affen weisen"; a 17th-century idiom meaning to make a fool of someone or lead them on a wild goose chase.</note> the following day. Therefore, he returns to him the following day, as he had promised, and after they had inspected the <term>Stein Lazuli|Lapis lazuli, a deep-blue stone used both in jewelry and as a source for the pigment ultramarine; here, the nobleman claims it contains silver.</term> and spoken about it together, the Scottish nobleman asks the apothecary if he also has a good <term>Vitrum Antimonii|Glass of Antimony; a semi-transparent, reddish-brown glass made by roasting and melting antimony ore, used in medicine and alchemy.</term>. The apothecary has some brought to him, but the Scotsman pretends that it has not been prepared according to the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the specific, often secret, procedures of alchemy.</note>. The apothecary excuses himself, saying that he did not make it himself nor have it made, but had purchased it already prepared from others, and he asks the nobleman then if he knows of a better preparation. The Scotsman says yes, and that he would very willingly and gladly teach him a better preparation, but only if the apothecary would lead him to a goldsmith and perform it there before the bellows. The apothecary takes note of this and sends his son, who was already bearded <note>Meaning the son was an adult, not a child.</note>, with him to the goldsmith; the son carried the crushed <term>Spießglaß|Antimony; a brittle, silvery-white metalloid that was a central substance in alchemical experimentation.</term>. They arrive at the goldsmith, whose name was Hans Loendorp, who lived in the neigh—

### Antimony turns to Gold.

Now, when the antimony had melted well, and the goldsmith wanted to pour it out onto a smooth grinding stone, as is customary—expecting to obtain the <term>Vitrum|Glass; specifically 'Glass of Antimony,' a translucent material resulting from the calcination and melting of antimony.</term> as he had understood from the apothecary's son—the Scottish nobleman says: "It is not necessary for you to pour it onto the stone; instead, pour it into an <term>Inguß|An ingot mold; a cast or frame used to shape molten metal as it cools.</term>." The goldsmith did as he was commanded, but saw that it had become **Gold**. At this, everyone in the house who was with him was not a little amazed and terrified. For besides the goldsmith and the apothecary’s son, two journeymen stood by and watched. The goldsmith did not know what to make of it—whether he was bewitched, or whether his eyes were deceiving him so that he could not see clearly what he had made and placed into the fire, or whether the apothecary intended to deceive and vex him. Therefore, he says to the Scotsman: "I saw that you still have half of the powder,"

No fraud could be found. ...because, when one casts gold through <term>Antimonium|Antimony; in this period, it refers to the mineral stibnite. Also, "sophistical" <note>Sophistical gold refers to "fake" or imitation gold—alloys or surface treatments that look like gold but do not pass the tests of weight or chemistry.</note> gold or silver—where silver is given a gold color, or copper is given a silver color—is not made through <term>Projection|The final step of the alchemical process, where a tiny amount of the "Philosopher's Stone" or Tincture is thrown into molten base metal to instantly transform it into gold or silver.</term> (as it is called in the Art), but through <term>Cement|Cementation; a refining process where metal plates are layered with powdered chemicals and heated.</term>, <term>Digerirung|Digestion; a process of slow, gentle heating intended to mature or "ripen" a substance.</term>, and many other similar preparations.

If one *Loht* of this tincture were to be thrown upon the aforementioned metals by weight, then one *Loht* would <term>tingiren|Tinge; the alchemical term for the tincture "coloring" or transforming the internal nature of a base metal into a noble one.</term> two thousand, eight hundred and twenty *Loht*; in coinage, this would amount to twenty-two thousand, five hundred and sixty <term>Rthl.|Reichsthaler; a large silver coin used as a standard currency throughout the Holy Roman Empire.</term>. From this, it is to be understood that this tincture was a mighty and high Work, which I should mention here for the benefit of the reader.

## The Fifth History.

Should then the experiments of the Apothecary of Treviso <note>Treviso is a city in the Veneto region of Italy.</note> and others be set aside and called lies? He who, in the presence of the Duke of Venice <note>The Doge of Venice.</note> and many councilors, transformed living mercury into the finest gold, as Girolamo Cardano <note>An influential Italian polymath (1501–1576) known for his work in mathematics and medicine.</note> testifies that it truly happened.

## The Sixth History.

Doctor Nicolaus Bernardus also recounts a history in his little book, in which he interprets and explains the Alchemical Epitaph <note>A famous and cryptic inscription known as "Aelia Laelia Crispis," often associated with alchemical secrets.</note> seen in Bologna. After Anselmus de Boodt <note>Anselmus de Boodt (1550–1632) was a Flemish humanist and physician to Emperor Rudolf II, famous for his work on gemstones.</note>—a Dutchman and Doctor of Medicine who was previously very much opposed to alchemists—had his father’s library brought from the Netherlands to Prague, he found among them (as Doctor Bernardus first pointed out to him) an old book written on parchment with the title *Cymbalum Aureum* <note>Latin: "The Golden Cymbal."</note>. As he wished to have it rebound in new parchment because the wooden boards were broken, he discovered by chance that it was hollow in the middle, covered only with parchment. Inside he found the process for the <term>lapidem|Latin: "stone," referring to the Lapis Philosophorum.</term>, or the Philosopher’s Stone, and a red powder weighing as much as a grain of barley, along with a description of how one should perform the <term>projection|The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the Stone or powder is "projected" onto molten base metal to transform it into gold.</term> with it. (See <term>Theatrum Chemicum|A massive compendium of alchemical writings first published in the early 17th century.</term> volume 3, pages 787 and 797, at the end).

### The Seventh History.

Even to this day, among the heirs of the noble and highly learned Master **Thaddaeus Hagecius** <note>Thaddaeus Hagecius (1525–1600), also known as Tadeáš Hájek, was a prominent Czech physician and the personal astronomer to Emperor Rudolf II.</note>, Doctor of Medicine—specifically with his son **Simeon**, a court servant to His Imperial Roman Majesty—a gold cake weighing twelve <term>lots|A historical unit of weight used in central Europe, typically equaling about 15 to 17 grams.</term> is to be found. Many honest people have seen it, and it may still be seen today. ## The Eighth History.

It is also still in the fresh memory of many noble lords and people—and it should rightly soften and move the stubborn attackers of alchemy—the experiments of **Edward Kelley** <note>Edward Kelley (1555–1597) was an English spirit medium and alchemist who worked closely with John Dee. He famously claimed to have discovered the secret of transmuting base metals into gold before falling out of favor with Emperor Rudolf II.</note>, an Englishman, which he demonstrated very often and many times in **Prague**, though to his own misfortune, as is known to many there. This is true even if he did not make the **tincture** <note>The "tincture" or "powder of projection" was the substance alchemists believed could catalyze the transformation of base metals into gold.</note> himself, but rather one of his countrymen, who has sufficiently shown in a book what he experienced in alchemy.

## The Ninth History.

**Giovanni Francesco Pico** <note>Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533) was an Italian philosopher who wrote *De auro* (On Gold), a defense of alchemy based on historical and legal evidence.</note>, a Count of **Mirandola**, also recounts many stories and examples in his book that gold and silver were made by alchemists, which are certainly not to be considered fables, but are truthful. However, those who attack alchemy are such malicious people that they will give no trust or belief to any man, and only desire that they alone should be believed.

## The Tenth History.

It is undeniable that even at the **Electoral Saxon Court** <note>The court at Dresden under Elector Augustus of Saxony was one of the most active centers for alchemical research in Europe during the late 16th century.</note>, metals were often altered and transformed into gold.

### The Twelfth History

There was also in Cologne a Frenchman, a Doctor of Medicine whose name was very well known, who transmuted copper into good silver. Many honest people were present for this, as told by Mr. Antonius Rinck—a prominent city councilor in Cologne—stating that it occurred without any false deception.

### The Thirteenth History

Even today, several people are known to me who "cook" and multiply silver through the addition of mercury <note>original: "Queck-silber," literally "living silver."</note> over a certain period of time. When they have reached a sufficient quantity, they take a portion, melt it, and sell the silver to the mint. This work of theirs is very difficult and excellent; I once saw the silver grow as high as a little finger, emerging from the earth just as asparagus shows itself in the spring <note>This likely refers to the "Silver Tree" or "Diana's Tree," a chemical reaction where silver crystals precipitate out of a solution, resembling plant growth.</note>.

deception and no Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the "Great Art" or "Sacred Art" of Alchemy.</note>: and that never through Nature and Art has another metal been transformed into gold. These **Alchemy-critics** <note>original: "Alchimisten Stürmer," literally "Alchemy-stormers," referring to those who aggressively attack or seek to debunk the practice.</note> pour out even more impudent speeches against the alchemists, writing of them: "They would surely come forward and make great mountains of gold to delight their eyes; indeed, they would enlist great armies, and no prince or king would be safe from them, and they would drive them out of their kingdoms." Indeed, I believe the earth would have to move and the heavens turn themselves around before such restless **new-sea-explorers** <note>A metaphorical jab at the "modern" skeptics of the era, likening them to those who restlessly seek new lands but miss spiritual or hidden truths.</note> would believe that metals could be changed. Oh, how blind and foolish those people are, that they take offense and are displeased because there is no **pride** <term>pride: original "Hoffahrt," meaning haughty arrogance or worldly vanity</term> among the true alchemists, but rather they desire only to be in secret, excluded from the world’s opulence and splendor. So, too, the alchemists do not concern themselves much when the unbelieving **Alchemy-critics** do not believe in the Art; instead, they endeavor all the more so that those who see do not see, and those who hear do not hear <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 13:13, suggesting that spiritual and alchemical truths are intentionally veiled from the unworthy.</note>. Only those whom **GOD** wishes to favor and give it to may understand, because they well understand what misfortune would follow if [the world] gave credence to the Art. 

This, my lover of Alchemy, which has now been written for you, I deem to be sufficient...

<vocab>Alchimisten, Gold, Metall, Alchymy, Alchimisten-Stürmer, Hoffahrt, GOtt</vocab>

<summary>This page defends alchemy against critics who claim it is a fraud, arguing that true alchemists remain hidden not out of inability, but to avoid worldly pride and social chaos. It emphasizes that alchemical knowledge is a divine gift intended only for the humble and secretive.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy-critics, transmutation, secrecy, pride, divine gift, spiritual alchemy, social order, defense of alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of alchemy, shifting from a rebuttal of "Alchemy-critics" mentioned on the previous page to a personal testimony. The author transitions from defending the possibility of the "Art" to promising practical chemical and medicinal recipes (the Tria Prima) in subsequent pages.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative printer's ornament consisting of three stylized floral or snowflake-like motifs arranged horizontally.</image-desc>

so that you may give credit that the Art of the **Transformation of Metals** <term>transformation: the alchemical process of transmutation, turning base metals into silver or gold</term> is certain; but others may be warned thereby to flee the cunning and fraud of the false, wandering alchemists. Should they, however, still refuse to desist, and allow themselves to be persuaded like those who are not wise, then I shall hereby be excused: For I have not written this so that others might be led astray and fall into ruin, but rather to show **God's wonder-works and gifts**, to recognize the truth, and to give public testimony of it, and so far as what I have seen myself in my living years, to which the **Alchemy-critics** <note>original: "Alchimisten Stürmer"</note> have incited me and likewise compelled me. I would have liked to present the friend of chemistry <note>original: "chimie Freund"</note> with several proven **medicines**; but because they are not yet all worked out, I will save them to be printed in a few pages.

Among these will be my discovery: the true **Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury** *from Vitriol* <note>original: "ex Vitriolo." These three substances, known as the Tria Prima, were considered the fundamental building blocks of all matter in Paracelsian alchemy.</note>, which *Jodocus von Rehe* <note>Jodocus von Rehe was a 16th-century legal scholar and alchemist known for his works on the transmutation of metals.</note> indeed described, but it does not work according to his description: These three parts, after transformation, are to be brought into the **highest medicine**, to serve one's neighbor therewith. Furthermore, from **tartar** <term>tartar: original "Weinstein," a potassium salt found in wine barrels, used as a chemical reagent</term>, **sal ammoniac**, and **crab’s eyes** <note>Crab's eyes (oculi cancrorum) were small, stony concretions found in the stomachs of crayfish, traditionally ground into powder and used as an antacid or for calcium.</note>, along with **dew**, to create an excellent medicine, through which all stone-related ailments, constipation of the body, the mesentery <note>original: "Gekrös"</note> and small veins, **dropsy** <term>dropsy: "Wassersucht," a historical term for swelling caused by excess fluid (edema)</term>, and **scurvy** <term>scurvy: "Schorbock"</term> can be happily relieved.

<summary>The author asserts the reality of metal transmutation while warning against fraudulent alchemists, promising to later reveal his own successful recipes for the "Tria Prima" and various medicines for common ailments.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, alchemy fraud, Tria Prima (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury), Vitriol, Jodocus von Rehe, medical recipes, dropsy, scurvy, tartar</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface and his promise of medical revelations. It follows the previous page's discussion of the "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) derived from minerals like vitriol, now expanding the scope to include medicines derived from animals.</meta>

<image-desc>Three floral printer's ornaments (fleurons) arranged in a horizontal row at the top of the page.</image-desc>

And then to create from **animal substances** <term>animal substances: in early modern medicine, materials derived from the animal kingdom were processed alongside minerals and plants to create potent remedies</term> a **tincture** <term>tincture: a concentrated medicinal extract, often considered the refined "spirit" or essence of a substance</term> which is intended to healthily cure all **epidemic diseases** <note>original: "epidemidische Kranckheit"</note> as well as poisonous **pestilential fevers** <note>original: "pestilentialische Fieber"</note>, etc. Now, just as the power and effect of these aforementioned works shall be described, every seeker shall also receive the **processes** <term>processes: the specific laboratory instructions or recipes used to prepare alchemical or chemical substances</term> who requests them from me. I wish herewith in conclusion that, just as this was promoted to print by me with good intentions, so also it may be received and accepted by **lovers of the art** <note>original: "Liebhabern." In this context, it refers to "amateurs" or "devotees"—those who love and practice the chemical arts for their own sake</note>, whom I commend to God.

->T H E   E N D<-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut tailpiece (cul-de-lampe) featuring symmetrical, ornate floral scrolls and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the author's introductory remarks, offering to share specific laboratory recipes for animal-based medicines against the plague and other epidemics. It reflects the early modern transition from alchemical secrecy to the sharing of "processes" with fellow practitioners.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the conclusion of the preface where the author offered laboratory recipes for animal-based medicines. It represents a physical transition in the manuscript, likely serving as a decorative divider or endpaper within the binding.</meta>

[Decorative Illustration]

This page features a decorative **marbled paper** <term>marbled paper: paper decorated with a veined or mottled pattern resembling marble, created by floating pigments on a liquid surface and "lifting" the pattern onto the paper</term> design, a staple of historical **bookbinding** <term>bookbinding: the artisanal craft of assembling a book and securing its pages within a protective cover</term>. 

The design is an intricate **snail pattern** <note>also commonly called a "curl" or "cockle" pattern</note>, characterized by rhythmic, wave-like swirls. These patterns are rendered in shades of dusty blue over a cream-colored base. In early modern manuscripts, such **endpapers** <term>endpaper: a leaf of paper at the front or back of a book, used to attach the text block to the cover boards</term> served both to protect the inner pages and to signify the owner's status or the quality of the work.

<summary>This page features a decorative marbled paper with a blue "snail" pattern, common in historical bookbinding. It marks a physical or section break within the manuscript's structure.</summary>
